* TODO Flipping a span's timestamps to active is hand-editing; give the review buffer a key :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       01849bef-fdc9-4c30-b467-00ca8606e8e0
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 08:52]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-26 Wed 08:52] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
:END:

*Requested by the user 2026-08-26:* "consider adding a keystroke to org-review that flips an inactive interval to active. that edit is tedious for me."

*What is tedious, concretely.* A span is rendered with *inactive* timestamps, and turning =[2026-08-26 Wed 08:12]--[2026-08-26 Wed 08:31]= into the active form means editing four bracket characters by hand in two places, after the item has already been assigned. The review buffer has =e= for editing an interval's *times* but nothing for its *kind*.

*Why it is inactive today, and why the request is not simply a display preference.* =b8e6007a= (in DONE.org) settled that the queue records *agent* activity and the agenda must not absorb it, and the active branch was dropped from =--review-format-annotation= outright. So flipping to active is not a formatting choice -- it asserts *this interval was the human's attention*, which is a different quantity and the one thing the agenda should show.

*Which makes this the manual half of [[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]].* That heading proposes clocking the human's review attention from the review command itself. Until it exists, the human is doing by hand exactly what it would automate, and the same day this was requested the user observed they had "forgotten to represent my attention as active spans" -- so the tedium is already producing omissions, not just friction.

*Design questions worth settling before binding a key.*
- *Does the flip mean "this was mine" or merely "show this in the agenda"?* If the former, the marker should probably be recorded on the item rather than inferred from bracket style, and the rendering follow from it.
- *What does it apply to?* A single item, the marked set, or every span in a cluster. Marking a whole review pass as the human's attention in one keystroke is likely the real want.
- *Which key.* =a= is assign, =e= is edit-interval, =N= is note, =d= is dismiss. =t= (for *type*) and =A= (active) both look free; check the keymap before choosing rather than trusting this list.

*Do not let this quietly become [[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]]'s substitute.* A key that makes the manual path bearable reduces the pressure to automate it, and the automated version is strictly better -- it knows when the review command was running, which a human retrofitting brackets afterwards is reconstructing from memory.

*Correction [2026-08-26 Wed]: the premise above is stale.* "Until it exists, the human is doing by hand exactly what it would automate" was written while [[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]] was open. It is now =DONE= -- the review pass clocks itself onto =Review attention= ([[id:335dfa97-c1b0-4e98-804f-81495eb63f20][335dfa97]]), and the command *is* the event. The automated path exists, so this heading is no longer the only way the human's attention gets represented.

*Which sharpens this heading's own closing caution rather than retiring it.* "Do not let this quietly become 961f15b6's substitute" was a warning about a future; it is now a live constraint. A key that flips brackets by hand is *worse* than what already ships for any interval a review pass covers, because the pass knows when it was running while a human retrofitting brackets afterwards is reconstructing from memory. Binding the key for that case would be building the inferior half of a thing already built.

*So the question narrows to a real one: what attention is neither an agent turn nor a review pass?* Measured 2026-08-26 rather than supposed. The span =[2026-08-26 Wed 13:05]--[2026-08-26 Wed 13:22]= is backed by six guideposts and nothing else. =--span-work-runs= extracts *one minute* of agent work from it, correctly, since only a =resume= -> =pause= adjacency counts. The other fourteen minutes are the user reading a session report: real attention, on this project, captured by nothing. Not agent activity, so [[id:b8e6007a-ae13-4b13-bb2e-a5df39435af0][b8e6007a]] rightly keeps queue-derived spans out of the agenda; not a review pass, so 961f15b6's clock never opened.

*That gap is the actual want behind the original request*, and it is worth separating from the tedium that prompted it. The user's words were "that edit is tedious for me" and, the same day, that they had "forgotten to represent my attention as active spans". The tedium argues for a key. The forgetting argues a key will not fix it either -- an omission caused by having to remember is not repaired by making the remembering cheaper.

*Two neighbours now carry pieces of this.* [[id:996fd2bd-be90-44b3-b2c5-9018255940b3][996fd2bd]] proposes reclaiming such a gap when an apply timestamp falls inside it, which is the automatic answer to the same question. [[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] records that the review buffer *names* an item by that 17-minute window while writing only the 1-minute payload -- so today the readout implies the gap is already accounted for, which is part of why it goes unnoticed.

*Still open and unchanged by any of this*: the three design questions above -- what the flip *means*, what it applies to, and which key.


* MAYBE Render a session transcript to readable org, so scrollback stops being the index :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Dev
:ID:       96ddf1ef-67fd-4d3c-b8cc-01ebb14a687d
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 08:48]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "MAYBE"      from              [2026-08-26 Wed 08:49] \\
  gated on whether /config resolves it
:END:

*Prompted 2026-08-26 by the user*: composing a prompt often means re-reading something far back in the session, and submitting jumps the view to the bottom, losing both the spot and the train of thought.

*The proximate cause is probably configuration, not this.* =~/.claude/settings.json= carries =tui: "fullscreen"=, which uses the terminal's alternate screen buffer -- the client owns the viewport and re-renders it, so the terminal's own scrollback is bypassed. Changing that setting via =/config= is the one-line experiment and should be tried first. This heading is the fix that survives whatever that turns out to be.

*The observation this rests on:* the transcript is already a plain file, at =~/.claude/projects/<project-slug>/<session-id>.jsonl=, and the user works in Emacs. Reading *there* while composing has no scroll position to lose, gives isearch and folding, and does not move when the agent answers.

*Why a renderer rather than just reading the JSONL.* It is one JSON object per line with content as an array of typed blocks; tool calls and their results dwarf the prose. Readable means: one heading per turn, prose as body, tool calls collapsed into a drawer so =#+STARTUP: content= folds them away by default.

*Already demonstrated to be worth having, twice in one session, both times by hand.* The footnote-discipline audit ([[id:2758f3a0-3c47-405e-9662-a7669dcb60cc][2758f3a0]]) replayed =bin/hooks/footnote-check='s logic over 84 assistant text blocks pulled out of this file. The sleep-window reconstruction ([[id:1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873][1a5a5254]]) cross-checked queue events against scratch-file mtimes. Both were one-off shell pipelines that were thrown away.

*Non-obvious detail worth capturing before it is rediscovered:* a =type: "user"= entry is *usually a tool result*, not a human turn. In this session's transcript, 218 of 231 were. Any renderer that treats them as prompts will report roughly twenty times too many turns -- which is exactly the error the footnote audit made on its first pass, reporting 81 of 83 blocks as end-of-turn before the filter was corrected to 11 of 84.

*=MAYBE= deliberately.* The =/config= change may resolve the pain entirely, and this costs a script plus its tests. Worth building only if reading the transcript in Emacs turns out to be something the user actually does, rather than something that sounds useful.


* TODO Settle what a slice proposal is, and write the procedure down :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Slices
:ID:       7c4d6ef6-c914-4d33-b267-cc9158a9f359
:CREATED:  [2026-08-25 Tue 15:49]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-25 Tue 15:49] \\
  extracted from the first proposal's body
:END:

*Extracted 2026-08-25 from the body of the first proposed slice*, where it had been written as though it were that proposal's own reasoning. It is not: it describes what *any* proposal is, which is the same error [[id:c44c2119-dbf8-4510-99e2-6aaa7f259d94][c44c2119]] was trimmed for hours earlier -- a slice's body carrying the convention rather than the instance. The user caught both.

*Done when the procedure is in* =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md= *beside the slice convention*, not when it is merely agreed here.

*What is settled already, by having done it once.* A proposal is a slice-shaped heading with the *declaration withheld*: the checklist, the theme and the ordering, but no =:KIND: slice=. Withholding is not bookkeeping -- declaring is the act of committing, so a heading without the declaration is precisely a proposal. It also keeps the checklist out of =bin/lint-org='s slice rules until someone means it; otherwise the absent =:BLOCKER:= would be an error against a list nobody has agreed to.

*And no prompt link*, for the same reason: there is no =next-session.md= revision driving a proposal, and there will not be until it is picked up.

*What accepting one means, which follows but has not been exercised:* add =:KIND: slice=, run =claude-code-ide-org-refresh-slice= so the blocker and cookie appear, and link the prompt revision that picks it up. Three steps, all mechanical, and worth a single command if proposals become routine.

*Genuinely open.* Where proposals live -- under =* Slices= beside real ones, as they are now, or somewhere that cannot be mistaken for a live slice at a glance. What keyword a proposal carries: =TODO= today, though =MAYBE= would read more honestly and would also stop [[id:42808717-2736-4bf4-872e-887f990203ca][42808717]]'s promotion trigger from ever nominating one as a next action. And what becomes of a rejected proposal -- =CANCELLED= keeps the argument, which is usually the part worth keeping.


* TODO Name the work a slice forgot: detect the omission, never fix it :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Slices
:ID:       c60a1c53-ba7f-40a2-979a-6ce0a16ce3c8
:CREATED:  [2026-08-25 Tue 15:35]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-25 Tue 15:35] \\
  the user had to remind me; a mechanism should
:END:

*Prompted 2026-08-25 by the user asking whether they have to remind me to add a worked heading to the open slice.* They did. [[id:0acc1df2-6916-4180-867a-99bfa551806b][0acc1df2]] was built, committed, and left out of [[id:c44c2119-dbf8-4510-99e2-6aaa7f259d94][c44c2119]] -- against a membership rule written earlier the same day, by me, saying a member is anything the slice *committed to or actually worked*.

*It must not add anything, and that is the design rather than caution.* Membership is what a slice *declares*; it is the entire difference between a slice and an epic, which is derived. A mechanism that swept in every heading touched would produce a backlog wearing a slice's clothes, which is the specific thing the user asked to resist while this slice finishes. So the job is to make forgetting *loud*, not to make remembering unnecessary.

*Choosing the signal is the whole problem, and it was measured rather than guessed.* Three candidates, against today as the test case:

| signal                                   | candidates | catches today |
|------------------------------------------+------------+---------------|
| state changed during the apply            | --         | *no*          |
| id appears anywhere in a commit since the slice opened | 27 | yes    |
| id appears in a commit *subject*          | 3          | yes           |

*The state-change signal fails on the case that prompted this*, which is what makes it worth recording. Nothing was queued for 0acc1df2 -- no keyword moved, no clock ran. It was built, and building leaves no trace the queue can see.

*Commit-body mentions are useless at 27*, because this project's commit prose cites ids constantly as cross-references. A report that lists 27 things stops being read, which is [[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]]'s finding arriving again.

*A subject line is a claim that the commit is **about** that heading*, and it narrows to three. Today's own commit reads "Build the slice regenerator: 0acc1df2". Cross-references stay in the body where they belong and are excluded for free.

*Where it runs: the end of a review pass.* Two reasons rather than one. A human is already there making per-item decisions, so asking costs nothing extra; and the =--review-settle-auto-promote= seam already exists for exactly this shape -- something that must run once, after the batch, against the settled state.

*Open, and worth deciding before building:* whether the report should also fire when *no* slice is open, which would be a different and probably more annoying question -- "you did work and no slice claims it" is sometimes just true.

*The same signal answers a second question, found 2026-08-25 when the user asked whether the session's work was attached to any task at all.* It was not: 52 state changes queued that day and *zero* clock events, the last having been 2026-08-24 16:42.

*The mechanism is visible in those two numbers.* Every heading went =TODO= -> =DONE= in one step. That is not a listed transition -- the table has =DOING= -> =DONE=, =PLANNING= -> =DONE=, =REVIEW= -> =DONE= -- so skipping the clocked state means no rule was ever triggered to open a clock. The omission is structural rather than forgetful: nothing asks for a clock on a transition that was never made.

*So the detector proposed here generalises for free.* An id in a commit subject is evidence that the commit was *about* that heading. Cross that against the day's clock intervals and two questions fall out of one scan:

- *which worked headings no slice names* -- this heading's original subject; and
- *which worked headings carry no clock interval that day* -- the attribution gap.

*The second is the more valuable of the two, on the day's evidence.* A forgotten slice member is a bookkeeping error someone notices at review. Un-clocked work is invisible: it lands on the meta-work day node as an unassigned span, indistinguishable at review from planning, and the human attributing it has no way to tell that three of those hours were implementation on a specific heading.

*And it is exactly the residue [[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]] shipped to eliminate*, which is the sharp part. That heading made explicit clock pairs authoritative so attribution would stop needing a human -- and it is the first member of the slice during which the pairs stopped being produced at all. It closed the consumption half; the discipline half regressed behind it.

*Shipped 2026-09-01. Detects; adds nothing*, as this body required — the job was to make forgetting *loud*, not to make remembering unnecessary.

*The signal is the one this body measured its way to.* =--commit-subject-ids= scans commit *subjects* only. An id in a commit body is a cross-reference and this project's prose cites ids constantly; a subject line is a claim that the commit *is about* that heading. A second filter does the rest: a token is kept only if it prefixes a *known heading id*, which removes abbreviated commit SHAs — the obvious false positive that a bare hex scan would produce, and which this project's subjects genuinely carry.

*Runs at the end of a review pass* (=--review-settle-forgotten=), beside the slice and separation settle steps, and swallows its own errors: bookkeeping after the work must not turn a successful apply into a failed one.

*The open question is answered: it reports only while a slice is open.* With none, "you did work and no slice claims it" is simply true, and a report firing on a true and unactionable fact is the prompt fatigue this project keeps designing against.

*Two defects the tests and the live run caught, and one of them was mine twice over:*

- *A dead flag.* An =open= boolean sat beside =earliest=, which is only ever set inside the open-slice branch — so it already encoded the answer and no mutation could change the result through it. Untested by construction, found by a mutation that failed nothing.
- *A vacuous assertion.* The first test asserted a member is *not* reported, in a fixture where no commit named the member — so it passed whether or not the exclusion existed. Same shape as the =move-sibling= assertion caught on 2026-08-31, and caught the same way.
- *And on the first live run: the slice reported itself.* Commit subjects cite the slice constantly ("add X to b36e6369"), and a slice never lists itself as a member, so without an explicit exclusion every open slice names itself forever. [[id:0086614a-c95c-4618-bba0-c821589dd7f2][0086614a]] excludes =self= for exactly this reason; this did not.

*Verified:* 491 tests before, 495 after, 0 unexpected. Five mutations, each failing only its own test — scanning bodies, dropping the known-id filter, treating closed slices as open, not excluding members, and not excluding the slice itself.

*The live result is zero, and the reason is worth recording because it is not a null result.* Of 95 commit subjects since the slice opened, *34* carry an 8-hex token — but every one resolves to either a declared member or a heading now listed as *incidental* by [[id:0086614a-c95c-4618-bba0-c821589dd7f2][0086614a]], and =--slice-members= reads incidental lines too. So the twins compose: one absorbs the work the slice did not plan, and this one is left reporting what neither accounts for.

*Which narrows what this heading uniquely catches, and that should be said plainly rather than glossed.* Incidentals cover *closed* work. A heading named in a commit subject, still *open*, and not a member is the residue — genuinely "built and left out", which is the [[id:0acc1df2-6916-4180-867a-99bfa551806b][0acc1df2]] case this was filed for. For closed work, 0086614a now answers first.

*Not built: the second question this body proposed* — which worked headings carry no clock interval that day. It shares the scan and this body rates it the more valuable of the two. It is a different report with a different audience and it should not be smuggled in beside this one; filed separately rather than left in prose.



* TODO Recover a close time from git for the headings with no in-file evidence :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Clock
:ID:       b7b46a26-2fd6-4784-bed3-d0112a917c0a
:CREATED:  [2026-08-24 Mon 10:14]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-24 Mon 10:20] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
- State "CANCELLED"  from "TODO"       [2026-08-24 Mon 15:59] \\
  nothing consumes CLOSED: on those headings
- State "TODO"       from "CANCELLED"  [2026-08-28 Fri 11:38] \\
  cancellation ground void: a consumer exists
:END:

*Filed 2026-08-24, the residue of* [[id:f4b07fc0-187a-4975-aa3b-b9bd4b1c0799][f4b07fc0]]. That pass filled 58 of DONE.org's 97 finished headings from their own =:LOGBOOK:=. *39 have no in-file evidence at all* -- mostly early headings from before the =!= cookies in =#+TODO:= were adopted, e.g. "Implement the ten pending MCP-tool tasks in parallel worktrees", "Add a PLANNING TODO state, driven by the ExitPlanMode matcher", and the original =org_query= / =org_clock_report= / =org_capture= tool headings.

*The one honest source left is git.* The commit that first shows the heading carrying a done keyword is real evidence, reachable with =git log -S= over the heading's =:ID:= line or its title. Nothing else in the tree knows.

*Two things to settle before building, and they are the whole of this heading:*

1. *It dates a different event, and the marker must not pretend otherwise.* A commit timestamp is when the change was *recorded*, not when the work stopped -- and in this project those can be far apart, since state changes reach the file only when a human runs the review pass. A =CLOSED:= written from a commit is therefore an upper bound, not a measurement. Whether that is worth having is the open question, not a detail: =7771fc63= retired an entire feature on the finding that a plausible guess is harder to reject than no guess at all, and this is the same shape of decision.
2. *If it is worth having, the approximation has to be visible in the file*, or the next reader cannot tell these 39 from the 58 that are exact. A property recording the derivation -- or simply not using =CLOSED:= for them at all -- is the choice to make.

*Note the third option, which does not require this to be built:* leave the 39 blank and record explicitly that no close time is recoverable, so the gap is a stated fact rather than an absence somebody tries to fill again in six months. That was offered alongside the git approach and deferred rather than rejected.

*A hazard already measured, and it applies directly here.* f4b07fc0's pre-tool estimate said 59 rather than 58, because a body-wide scan matched a =- State "DONE" ...= line quoted *in prose* on [[id:3d93021d-1d8a-4657-bc5c-77734f91fb9d][3d93021d]], a heading that has no =:LOGBOOK:= and was diagnosing the bug that produced that line. Any git-side matching on heading text or on =State= lines in a diff faces the same trap, with no drawer boundary available to bound it.

*Cancelled 2026-08-24 by the user's decision, and the reasoning is the point rather than the verdict.*

The heading posed the question as "is a commit timestamp a good enough upper bound", and answering *that* would have taken the git archaeology to find out. It turns out not to be the deciding question.

*Nothing mechanical consumes =CLOSED:= on these 39 headings, and nothing ever will.* [[id:8bcd56f4-22a8-4673-bad6-a99433027b81][8bcd56f4]]'s lint rule is scoped to headings closed on or after 2026-08-24, so these are exempt *permanently, by design* -- the scoping is what makes that rule usable at all. Archive routing is per-category =:ARCHIVE:=, not datetree, so nothing files by =CLOSED:=. No clocktable reads it. The only consumer left is a human reading DONE.org, and 35 of the 39 already carry an =:ARCHIVE_TIME:=.

So the work would derive a date from a *different event*, write it into a field nothing reads, on headings in a file that CLAUDE.md says is reference and never orientation. That is the shape [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] settled against, arrived at from the other direction: not "the guess is unreliable" but "nothing is asking".

*The measurement that was expected to decide it, recorded because it did not:* the 39 are not uniformly pre-cutover. By =:ARCHIVE_TIME:= they split 19 / 3 / 13 / 4 across 2026-07-29, 07-30, 08-17 and none. A pre-cutover close is tightly bounded by its commit, since =org-todo= applied live; a post-cutover one is bounded only by when a human next ran the review pass, which today reached back two weeks. So the bound would have been tight for some and arbitrarily loose for others, with nothing in the file to say which -- a worse outcome than a uniform absence.

*Option (c) is also declined,* for the same reason rather than a different one: marking 39 headings as "no close time recoverable" costs 39 property writes to record an absence that is already visible as an absence. The absence is not ambiguous here -- there is no "nobody looked" reading to resolve, which is what made that distinction worth paying for on [[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]].

*What would reopen this:* a consumer appearing. If archive filing ever moves to a datetree ([[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]]'s question), =CLOSED:= becomes load-bearing for placement and these 39 become genuinely undatable rather than merely undated.

*Decided by the user 2026-08-31: option B.* Write =CLOSED:= from git for the 39 headings that have none, and mark each one as derived.

*Both of this heading's original questions are now answered, and by different means.* Whether a commit-derived =CLOSED:= is worth having: yes — every consumer uses it as a placement key, a threshold or a presence test, so nothing mechanical is misled, and the enumeration above is the evidence. How the approximation is marked: with a property, which keeps all three consumers untouched because none of them looks at it.

*The property is =:CLOSED_SOURCE: git=*, absent on the 58 measured ones. Checked before coining: no derivation marker exists in either file today, and the asymmetry is the right way round — a measured =CLOSED:= needs no annotation, so only the 39 carry a line.

*Implementation, mostly already done once.* [[id:38b92521-bba4-4bb5-9cd9-465862471a72][38b92521]]'s completion-time manifest computed git-derived times for 13 of the 39 on 2026-08-17, via =git log --reverse -S= over the heading line, and used them to order the archive sweep. Those 13 can be lifted from that table rather than recomputed. The remaining 26 are the project's earliest headings — the five original tool headings, the hook and trigger batch, =Set git identity= — and predate the =!= cookies, so git is genuinely the only source.

*One trap already found and paid for, recorded in that manifest and worth not rediscovering:* the in-file scan must be scoped to a heading's *own* body. Scanning the subtree dates a parent from one of its children — =b5f7c5c7= came out two days early that way, and it would have mis-ordered the two largest entries with nothing downstream to notice.

*What this unblocks:* [[id:33864a0f-5e13-4235-85a6-0bd9d2fe5252][33864a0f]], which is blocked on this alone. Without it, =org-archive-subtree= would date all 39 from =time= — today — and pile them into a single wrong day node, silently.

*Every consumer of =CLOSED:= enumerated 2026-08-31, because the decision hinges on whether any of them reads it as a measurement.* Three, and none does:

| consumer | how it uses the value |
|----------+-----------------------|
| =org-archive-subtree= (org-archive.el:294) | =(or (org-entry-get nil "CLOSED" t) time)= — the datetree *placement key* |
| =bin/lint-org='s =:PLAN:= rule (config.el:9491) | compared against =claude-code-ide-org-plan-drawer-since= — a *threshold* |
| [[id:f4b07fc0][f4b07fc0]]'s backfill (config.el:9953) | tests *presence*; never reads the value |

*So a value wrong by hours or a day breaks nothing mechanical.* It lands in the right day node or occasionally the adjacent one; it falls on the correct side of a threshold set weeks earlier; it satisfies a presence test. The machine case for refusing an approximation does not exist.

*Which narrows the question to the human, and that is exactly [[id:7771fc63][7771fc63]]'s ground.* That heading retired a guess because "a plausible suggestion is harder to reject than no suggestion at all". The 39 approximations would be indistinguishable from the 58 measurements, permanently, for any reader.

*And the marker costs almost nothing.* Option B — write =CLOSED:= plus a property recording the derivation — leaves all three machine consumers untouched, since none of them looks at the extra property, and gives the human the one thing 7771fc63 says matters. Thirty-nine extra lines, all of them in DONE.org.

*This heading's own second question already assumed marking* — "how the approximation is marked so a reader can tell the 39 from the 58 that are exact" — so B is not a new option, it is the one the heading was already reaching for before it knew the consumers were indifferent.

*What is left to decide is therefore one thing only:* whether the human-facing marker is wanted, given that nothing mechanical needs it. Everything else about this heading is now measured.

*Shipped 2026-09-01 as option B. Every finished heading in both files now carries a =CLOSED:=; the derived ones say so.*

*The count matched this body exactly:* 39 in DONE.org, plus 2 in TODO.org that it could not have known about — finished children of *live* parents, which the archive sweep correctly leaves in place. 41 filled, *0 skipped*: git had evidence for every one.

*The upper-bound claim was measured rather than assumed, and it holds without exception.* Against the 60 headings whose =CLOSED:= is known exactly, the git-derived time was later in *60 of 60* cases — never earlier. Median 72 minutes late; worst case about 49 hours. So the derivation cannot invent a close *before* the work, which is the property that makes it safe for all three consumers: it lands in the right day node or occasionally the adjacent one, falls on the correct side of a threshold set weeks earlier, and satisfies a presence test.

*The pickaxe runs over both files at once, and that is load-bearing rather than convenient.* Archiving moves a heading line from TODO.org to DONE.org, and a move *inside one pathspec* nets zero occurrence change — so the archive commit does not match and the first hit is the commit that genuinely introduced the line. Scoping to one file would have dated 35 of these headings from their archive instead of their close.

*=:CLOSED_SOURCE: git= on all 41, and nothing else touched.* DONE.org's diff is 78 lines *added and none removed* — two per heading. The asymmetry this body specified is preserved: a measured =CLOSED:= carries no annotation, so only the derived ones are marked, and the 164 exact ones in DONE.org are untouched.

*Idempotent, and the test for that is the one that matters:* a heading which already has =CLOSED:= is skipped whatever its source, so a second run cannot overwrite a *measured* close with an upper bound. That is the single outcome that would make the file worse, and a mutation removing the guard fails that test alone.

*Verified:* 495 tests before, 498 after, 0 unexpected. Three mutations, each failing only its own test — dropping the derivation marker, dropping idempotency, and letting the dry run write.

*One weakness of my own found by that third mutation, and worth recording because it is a shape rather than a slip.* The dry-run test originally checked only *disk*, and the save is separately guarded — so a dry run that edited the buffer without saving passed it. A dry run that leaves a dirty buffer is not harmless: a human can save it by accident, and =--file-busy-p= then reads the file as busy. The test now asserts =buffer-modified-p= is nil.

*What this unblocks:* [[id:33864a0f-5e13-4235-85a6-0bd9d2fe5252][33864a0f]], which was blocked on this alone. Without it =org-archive-subtree= would have dated all 41 from =time= — today — and piled them into a single wrong day node, silently.

:END:

*Observed in the applied record 2026-08-22.* [[id:82df2a6c-881d-4117-a752-5b3b04c9656d][82df2a6c]] carries =CLOCK: [2026-08-21 Fri 14:53]--[15:42] => 0:49=, and the day node =2026-08-21 Friday= carries four intervals -- 15:06--15:07, 15:11--15:13, 15:25--15:30, 15:34--15:35 -- every one of them *inside* that window. About nine minutes is clocked against two headings at once.

Both items were offered in the same review buffer and both were correct in isolation: one an assigned span, the other the suggested rendering of an explicit =clock_in=/=clock_out= pair. Nothing checked them against each other.

*Not a rounding concern.* The second co-equal goal of this project is a trustworthy record of where attention went, and a clocktable that sums to more than the elapsed day is the plainest possible violation of it. A remainder-style guard already exists for editing one item's interval; the missing check is across items in one batch.

*Related and distinct:* [[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]] would make explicit pairs authoritative, which shrinks this problem by construction -- a partitioned span cannot overlap the pair that partitioned it -- but does not remove the need for the check.

*Retitled 2026-08-28, because the old title had been overtaken.* It read "A span and an explicit clock interval can both apply, double-counting the minutes", which names the general phenomenon — and the same-heading instance of it was fixed by =--subtract-intervals=. The index was therefore advertising a solved problem, and the natural reading of the title was the fixed one.

What survives is the case this body already documents and subtraction structurally cannot reach: the two overlapping intervals live in *different* =:LOGBOOK:= drawers. =--subtract-intervals= reads the target heading's own drawer, so an interval on 82df2a6c and one on the day node are each correct in their own drawer and invisible to each other. The check that is missing is across items in one batch, not within one heading — which is what the body said before the fix and still says.

The =/next-session= idiom is used but not codified. One line outside the file mentions it -- =CLAUDE.md:126= -- and it documents the *artifact* while explicitly denying it is a convention: "a plan, not a convention". Nothing states when one is written, what it must contain, or what "consumed" means.

*First assertion: every item states whether it carries an unresolved decision, and whose it is.* The 2026-08-21 prompt did this correctly for its item 5 ("the user decides; do not pick silently") and not for item 1, where "Possible shapes, none chosen" sat in the heading body instead -- so the executing session chose a shape with no checkpoint. That is the gap in one sentence: the prompt planned at the *sequencing* altitude (survey, slice, order, cut line) and not at the *design* altitude, and only the second needs a marker.

*Evidence this is a recurring class rather than one slip.* =* Documentation= already holds six headings of the "a convention is nowhere written down" shape: [[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]], [[id:9651d4c8-6887-4b0a-91d9-562c3bc49a75][9651d4c8]], [[id:241a4c54-ed59-4780-b602-2a86efee978a][241a4c54]], [[id:bba1088c-d8e1-49f7-bef8-b070860f5257][bba1088c]], [[id:8707de36-93a7-4e34-b4ba-48008c7e1e3b][8707de36]] and this one. [[id:d2a0f54c-b59a-4855-ae3d-c93a6d3aa177][d2a0f54c]] is the mechanical answer to the class and would check this format once it exists.

*Home: a new user-scoped skill, not the org-dev skill.* org-dev's trigger is changing this repo's own code and verifying it took effect; writing a prompt at end-of-session is neither, so the rule would silently never load -- the same failure CLAUDE.md names for path-scoped rules. And the idiom is org-free, Emacs-free and repo-free, which makes it the most portable thing here. [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] measured that only =~/.claude/= travels; this is its easiest first instance, a skill with *zero* repo coupling against the org skill's measured 16 lines. The format can additionally self-host in the prompt file's own header, which needs no trigger at all.

*Open:* there is no end-of-session trigger to hang the writing rule on. Candidate is firing on an explicit request to write or revise the prompt, with the self-hosted header as the backstop.

The ritual: a standing daily prompt to actually do review and planning.

*It reminds; it does not create.* Creating today's node is [[id:9575e65b-b589-465a-830c-286541780f96][9575e65b]]'s job and happens when work happens. A repeater that created the node instead would manufacture one per calendar day whether or not anything occurred, and re-couple the node's id to a schedule -- both of the things the on-demand resolver exists to avoid.

*A category is the one place in this repo a repeater is safe* ([[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]]): a repeater never reaches DONE, so it can never sit under a completable ancestor, and a category carries no keyword and completes nothing.

* TODO Comma-escape headline lines when documenting org structure inside a body :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Skill
:ID:       8a23d6ec-ca45-454d-b65f-e46ea9204816
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 10:03]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-21 Fri 10:03] \\
  document the escape, decide on enforcement
CLOCK: [2026-08-21 Fri 10:35]--[2026-08-21 Fri 10:37] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-21 Fri 10:35]--[2026-08-21 Fri 10:37] evaluate inline src_org for snippets
:END:

Proposed by the user 2026-08-21, while asking for a materialized example of a proposed subtree: if showing org structure inside a heading body works, make it a general habit rather than a one-off, since it would also help linting.

*It works, and the mechanism is not the one the question assumed.* The block type is irrelevant. Measured over four variants, counting what =org-map-entries= returns from a buffer holding one real heading, an example block, and a second real heading:

| block            | escaped | org-map-entries sees                        |
|------------------+---------+---------------------------------------------|
| =#+BEGIN_SRC org=  | no      | Real, *Fake A*, *Fake B*, Second            |
| =#+BEGIN_SRC org=  | =,*=      | Real, Second                                |
| =#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE=  | no      | Real, *Fake A*, *Fake B*, Second            |
| =#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE=  | =,*=      | Real, Second                                |

=SRC= and =EXAMPLE= behave identically in both columns. *The comma escape is the whole mechanism*, and execution has nothing to do with it -- disabling evaluation would not have helped, because the risk is the outline, not the code.

*Unescaped, example org is not example org -- it is real headings.* A raw =* Fake A= inside a block is walked into by =org-map-entries= and matched by a bare =^\*+ = regex alike, so it would corrupt =org_outline=, =claude-code-ide-org--container-heading-p= (a body full of examples would make any heading a container), the statistics cookies derived from that, and =bin/lint-org= -- each silently, and each in a way that looks like a content bug rather than a formatting one.

*So the rule is "comma-escape headline lines", not "use example blocks".* Stating it the second way is what would make it fail: someone reaches for a block, gets the syntax highlighting they wanted, and inherits the defect the block never protected against.

*Related precedent already in the suite.* =claude-code-ide-org-test--decoy-heading= pins that a *drawer*-shaped line inside =#+begin_example= is not a drawer, and =claude-code-ide-org--find-drawer= skips it. Headings are the case that was never covered, and they behave the opposite way -- the parser is not consulted, so the block does not protect them.

*Where this should land if adopted:* =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md=, which is path-scoped to =**/*.org= and already owns the file-shape conventions. A first specimen is live in [[id:25ec37b0-460f-404d-be83-8a4b4d355cdc][25ec37b0]]'s body.

*Open:* whether =bin/lint-org= should *enforce* it -- flagging an unescaped headline line inside a block -- or whether documenting it is enough. Enforcement is attractive because the failure is silent, but it needs care: a block quoting a *diff* or a shell transcript may legitimately contain a line starting with =*=.

*Inline =src_LANG{BODY}= was raised by the user 2026-08-21 as a possible companion for short snippets, and as another aid to linting. Measured, it is neither -- and on the linting question it cuts slightly the wrong way.*

*It cannot help, because the hazard it would guard against cannot occur inline.* The defect this heading exists for is a =*= at *column 0 on its own line*. An inline snippet sits inside a paragraph by definition, so its =*= is never at column 0. Confirmed alongside the alternatives -- =src_org{,* x}=, ==* asterisk== and =~* asterisk~= each left =org-map-entries= reporting exactly the two real headings. There is nothing here for an inline form to protect, and ===verbatim=== is equally safe and already this repo's convention.

*Where the hazard does live, inline src fails.* An attempted multi-line inline block leaked:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
src_org{,* one
,* two}
#+END_EXAMPLE

Unescaped, the second line's =*= is at column 0 and =org-map-entries= returned a heading titled =two}=. Inline forms cannot span lines, so they cannot express the multi-line structure that made this heading necessary.

*It adds an execution surface that ===verbatim=== does not have.* =org-babel-map-inline-src-blocks= finds =src_emacs-lisp{(+ 1 2)}= as one executable block, so anything that walks a buffer for babel -- =org-babel-execute-buffer=, export with default =:exports= -- treats it as code rather than as text. For files that tools read and rewrite constantly, that is a new hazard bought for no linting benefit. Disabling evaluation per block is possible and is exactly the kind of per-use precaution that gets forgotten once.

*So: reserve it for rendering, if these files are ever exported, and do not adopt it as a habit.* ===verbatim=== stays the convention for inline code, and the comma escape stays the rule for block-level structure. Two mechanisms, each for the case it actually addresses.

*One honest limit on the above.* Whether org parses these as =inline-src-block= / =verbatim= objects was *not* verified -- three attempts at an =org-element-map= probe returned nil even for ===verbatim===, which org certainly does parse, so the probe was broken rather than the syntax. The outline and babel results above stand on their own mechanisms and do not depend on it.


* TODO Decide how far the review-apply cycle can be automated, given the interactive constraint :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       361e2708-8124-4bd1-9c44-f97ca97aca4a
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 08:08]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-21 Fri 08:09] \\
  decide the automation boundary
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-21: the review-apply cycle "seems pretty straightforward lately", so how much of it still needs a human at all?

*It is three layers, and only one of them is actually walled.* Keeping them apart is the whole of this heading, because "automate the review" reads as one question and is not:

1. *Deciding what a span means* -- computing a heading for an unassigned interval. Pure computation over the queue and whatever external evidence is admitted; it touches no org buffer and no live Emacs state. Automatable in principle, and the thing standing in its way is evidence, not architecture -- see [[id:5a6d2c75-9a42-4dd4-b4d6-670aeebc5b46][5a6d2c75]].
2. *Confirming* -- the human keystroke per item. Not walled, just currently per-item.
3. *Writing* -- applying through native =org-todo=/=org-clock-in=/=org-clock-out=. This is the wall, and CLAUDE.md names it as load-bearing: org's native state-change logging only completes correctly inside a genuinely interactive command, and a non-interactive =emacsclient -e= hits the exact hang the queue exists to route around. The concrete mechanism is the =#+TODO:= cookies -- =WAITING(w@/!)= and =CANCELLED(c@)= carry =@= and *prompt for a note*, so those two keywords and only those two block a non-interactive apply.

*So "completely" is available for layer 1 and not for layer 3.* The honest target is that layer 2 collapses: a pass in which every item is unambiguous should be appliable with one confirmation for the whole pass rather than one per item, still inside =M-x claude-code-ide-org-review= so layer 3 keeps its interactive context. Nothing about that weakens the constraint -- the command is still human-triggered and still interactive.

*What has to be defined before any of it is built:* what "unambiguous" means mechanically. A rule that admits a bulk confirmation has to be one a human can trust without reading the items, which is a higher bar than the per-item readout meets today.

*The objection that binds, and its exact scope.* [[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]] records that a model reasoning over the queue file alone has *strictly less* information than the threshold algorithm -- the same timestamps and heading IDs, minus any domain knowledge -- and that a fluent proposal is harder to reject than a transparently mechanical one. That holds against any automation whose only input is the queue. It does not settle the case for an automation that reads the session transcripts, which carry what the queue does not.


* TODO An unassigned span's readout omits the evidence that would make its heading obvious :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       5a6d2c75-9a42-4dd4-b4d6-670aeebc5b46
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 08:08]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-21 Fri 08:09] \\
  add transcript evidence to span readout
:END:

Stated by the user 2026-08-21, as the one hitch left in an otherwise straightforward review pass: the readout "lacks adequate context of what was happening immediately before it to make the assignment obvious, but I believe it's there in the data."

*Not a re-file of [[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]].* That one shipped 2026-08-14 and put commits and heading creations inside an unassigned span's window on screen. It helped and did not close the gap; this is the next increment against the same complaint.

*Where the data is, and the distinction that decides the approach.* The queue carries timestamps, =session_id=, =agent_id=, =agent_type= and =source= -- and 0c8644ff's objection that this is *strictly less* than the threshold algorithm already has is correct and not disputed here. The session *transcripts* are a different corpus: [[id:420816ec-677e-45a5-b898-fb5db70d214b][420816ec]]'s research half read 69 of them. A transcript records what the session was asked and which files it touched in a given window, which is exactly the missing context, and extracting it is mechanical rather than a judgement call.

*Candidate evidence, cheapest first:* files touched in the window, taken from the transcript's tool calls; the prompt text that opened the enclosing turn; and, for the case where the competing activity was not this project at all, [[id:1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873][1a5a5254]]'s power assertions.

*Render evidence, do not recommend a heading.* "This session edited =config.el= around 10:20" is a fact the human weighs; "this span was probably c6fc6f46" is a proposal, and 0c8644ff's anchoring argument applies to the second and not the first. Holding that line is what keeps this heading independent of the automation decision in [[id:361e2708-8124-4bd1-9c44-f97ca97aca4a][361e2708]] rather than a prerequisite for it.

*Test corpus is already sitting in the queue:* three unassigned spans as of 2026-08-21 -- =16:28--16:54= and =17:18--17:22= on 08-20, =21:08--21:11= on 08-20. Whether the added evidence makes each one's heading obvious is answerable against those three before anything is generalised.

*Two cues the user asked for by name, 2026-08-22, after allocating a batch and only afterwards realising what it contained.* Both are about what the buffer *already knows* and does not show, which is this heading's subject rather than a new one.

*"Am I actually catching meta-work? I didn't even notice because there's no visual cue in the review dialog."* A span assigned to the =Review and planning= day node and a span assigned to an ordinary task look identical, so the one measurement the datetree exists to produce is invisible at the moment it is being made. The day node is mechanically distinguishable -- it is the =:DATE_TREE:= heading's descendant -- so this is a rendering decision, not an inference.

*Coverage, per span rather than per item.* Once [[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]] makes explicit =clock_in= pairs authoritative, most runs in a span will already have an owner. The buffer should say which: "12 of 15 runs attributed, 3 need you" turns an hour-long yes/no into three small local questions, and shows the human where their judgement is actually required. Rendered from the same data, and it is the difference between allocating and *confirming*.

*Why this pairing matters more than either alone.* The evidence this heading was filed about answers "which heading?" The coverage line answers the prior question -- "is this even mine to decide?" -- which on the measured day was the one that went wrong: an hour that the queue had already attributed was presented as a single undifferentiated choice.


* TODO Index a heading's git history from a drawer, so the body never has to narrate it :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Skill
:ID:       41cf5830-fc2e-4dc6-b243-a0c2718a53a3
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 11:07]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-20 Thu 16:44] \\
  captured keywordless; state never set
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-20 alongside the retention policy at [[id:3063c3e5-0dd0-4354-8985-271b0313c5d1][3063c3e5]], and separable from it: once a body may be revised wholesale, the evolution it used to carry lives only in git, and nothing points a reader at it.

Proposal: a timestamped commit log in a drawer -- possibly =:LOGBOOK:=, possibly its own -- listing the commits that touched this heading. Not the history itself, an index into it. The framing that makes it worth building is the user's: "how did we get here?" is a *rare* question, so the answer belongs one hop away rather than inline, which is precisely the trade the current bodies get backwards.

*The query, settled 2026-08-20 and measured.* The first proposal here was =git log -S<id>=, and it is wrong. Against =02aaae22=: of the 24 commits =-S= reports, *only 4 actually touched the heading*, and it misses 23 of the 27 that did. Both failures follow from what =-S= does -- it matches the ID string anywhere in the *file*, so a commit is reported merely for adding an =[[id:02aaae22...]]= link inside some other heading's body, while an edit to this heading's own prose never touches the =:ID:= line and so is invisible.

The user's formulation is the correct one: commits touching anything from the heading line to the character before the next heading. That is =git log -L <start>,<end>:TODO.org=, which follows the range backwards through history as the file changes. Locate the range at HEAD by =:ID:=, then hand the line numbers to =-L=.

Anchoring =-L= on a regex instead was tried and is worse: =/:ID:       <uuid>/= starts the range *below* the headline, losing retitles, tag edits and cookie updates -- 20 commits against the line range's 27, with no false positives. A title regex is worse still, since titles and statistics cookies both change.

*Range compression works but does not transform the problem.* Of 302 commits to TODO.org, 27 touched =02aaae22=; consecutive runs are 4, 3, 2, 2, 2, 2 and twelve singletons, so =a..b= notation gives 18 entries instead of 27 -- a third off.

*That number now argues against the drawer.* Eighteen entries for one heading, in a file whose largest containers will accumulate more, is not an index a human scans -- it is a second body, which is the thing this heading exists to prevent. Derived on demand still looks stronger, but on *volume* grounds rather than the staleness grounds first recorded here.

Remaining open: which drawer, if stored; and what generates it. A commit touching a heading is detectable at commit time, and =.githooks/pre-commit= is the only thing that runs then -- at the cost of making a lint hook a writer.

/Body restored 2026-08-20 by =org_amend=; the original capture silently discarded it -- see/ [[id:1cd2df62-35e9-47dc-bcce-dda7bae3c522][1cd2df62]]. /This heading being empty is how the loss was noticed at all: the user asked where the concept was parked, and there was nothing there./


* DOING [0/1] Review how the emacs-tools are advertised, against which ones get used :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Tools
:ID:       420816ec-677e-45a5-b898-fb5db70d214b
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 07:41]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-20 Thu 07:41] \\
  seven of thirteen tools never called
CLOCK: [2026-08-20 Thu 07:47]--[2026-08-20 Thu 07:52] =>  0:05
- [2026-08-20 Thu 07:47]--[2026-08-20 Thu 07:52] assigned at review
- State "TODO"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-20 Thu 07:49] \\
  re-queued; keyword was dismissed
CLOCK: [2026-08-20 Thu 07:57]--[2026-08-20 Thu 07:58] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-20 Thu 07:57]--[2026-08-20 Thu 07:58] assigned at review
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-08-20 Thu 21:10] \\
  measuring usage across 69 transcripts
CLOCK: [2026-08-21 Fri 07:49]--[2026-08-21 Fri 07:56] =>  0:07
- [2026-08-21 Fri 07:49]--[2026-08-21 Fri 07:56] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-21 Fri 07:59]--[2026-08-21 Fri 08:02] =>  0:03
- [2026-08-21 Fri 07:59]--[2026-08-21 Fri 08:02] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-21 Fri 08:43]--[2026-08-21 Fri 08:45] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-21 Fri 08:43]--[2026-08-21 Fri 08:45] assigned at review
:END:

Thirteen tools are advertised. Over a two-day session that did almost
nothing *but* org work, *seven were never called once*: =org_query=,
=org_refile=, =org_move_sibling=, =org_sort_children=,
=org_clock_report=, =org_archive=, =org_log_background_plan=.

*And the work they exist for was done anyway, by other means.* That is
the finding — not that they went unneeded, but that they went unreached:

| what happened                                    | tool not reached for |
|--------------------------------------------------+----------------------|
| cross-file searches, dozens of them              | =org_query=          |
| moved three subtrees between files and levels    | =org_refile=         |
| ordered a composed epic's children               | =org_sort_children=  |
| totalled clock time over the whole corpus        | =org_clock_report=   |

Each was done with =grep=, =python=, or hand-written =org-cut-subtree=
/=org-paste-subtree= elisp. In every case the tool would have been
shorter and safer; the hand-rolled subtree move is exactly what left
=org-id= stale in
[[id:8ddd7fa8-e847-42e9-b82c-63bbe4c11b7b][8ddd7fa8]].

*Four specific mis-advertisements, each with a cost already paid:*

1. =org_capture='s =target= is optional and omitting it writes *outside
   the repository*, to the capture file. The description does not say
   so. Cost: a heading filed where the user could not see it
   \([[id:97696fc2-f07b-4586-b280-de6a7d26fb87][97696fc2]]\).
2. =org_capture= can only target an =:ID:= or a top-level heading *of
   the capture file*, so filing under a real category is impossible —
   categories deliberately carry no =:ID:=, and =bin/lint-org= enforces
   that. The natural call cannot be expressed, so headings get
   hand-written instead.
3. =org_amend= only ever appends. Nothing says so at the call site, and
   it is the mechanism behind
   [[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]]: every summary
   and correction lands furthest from where a reader starts.
4. =org_set_todo= accepted a transition to the state already held, four
   times in one day, before =c2cf203= refused it.

*The question this heading asks* is not "are the descriptions accurate"
— mostly they are, and several are excellent. It is whether they are
written so a model reaches for them *at the moment of need*. A
description read once at session start competes with =grep=, which needs
no recall at all. What would change that is unproven: naming the
alternative it beats, stating the failure it prevents, or something
else.

*Measurement to repeat, since it is cheap:* the queue's =source= field
records every tool that queues, and this session shows =org_set_todo= 36,
=org_clock_out= 10, =org_clock_in= 9. The immediate tools leave no trace
at all, which is itself worth fixing before judging them — right now
"never used" and "used and forgotten" are indistinguishable for seven of
the thirteen.

*Measured 2026-08-20 across all 69 session transcripts in this project.* The measurement half of this heading is now done; what remains is the child and whatever follows from finding 4.

*1. The original premise held for a session and does not hold for the corpus.* Only *one* tool of fourteen has never been called in any session: =org_sort_children=. =org_query=, =org_refile= and =org_move_sibling=, named above as never called, have since been used -- 27, 14 and 2 times. The "seven of thirteen" observation was true of the two-day session it was made in, and that scoping is what makes it interesting rather than wrong.

*2. Per-session breadth is the real number, and it is low.* Of 20 sessions making 10 or more org tool calls, the median reaches for *5 distinct tools of 14*. No session used more than 8. So the surface is not unused; it is unused *at any given moment*.

*3. =org_amend= is the sharpest case of recall rather than utility.* 121 lifetime calls -- the third most-used tool -- yet absent from 16 of 20 active sessions. When it is remembered it carries a session; most sessions never think of it. Also absent from most active sessions: =org_log_background_plan= (19/20), =org_archive= (18/20), =org_refile= (18/20), =org_move_sibling= (18/20), =org_clock_report= (17/20).

*4. The finding that reframes this heading: a standing rule works and a good description does not.* CLAUDE.md requires calling =org_pending_updates= before the first state or clock call of a session. Compliance since that rule landed on 2026-08-17 is *4 of 4 sessions, 100%*; before it, 3 of 34, and those three were coincidence. Small n and worth re-measuring, but unambiguous so far.

Set against it, on the same day: =org_capture='s description states in capital letters that it writes NO TODO keyword and that =org_set_todo= must follow. That instruction was ignored five times in one session by a reader who had the description in context, losing four heading bodies ([[id:1cd2df62-35e9-47dc-bcce-dda7bae3c522][1cd2df62]]).

*So the title's framing may be wrong.* This heading asks how the tools are *advertised*. The evidence says advertising is not the lever that moves usage -- a directive in CLAUDE.md is, because it is read at session start rather than at the moment of a call that is never made. If under-use is worth fixing, the intervention to test is a standing rule naming the tool for a situation, not a better =:description=.

*What not to conclude.* Low breadth is not obviously a defect. Sorting a heading's children is genuinely rare, and =org_sort_children='s zero calls may be honest rather than a discovery failure. Deciding which of the six under-used tools deserve a rule, and which are simply niche, is the remaining judgement and is not answered by any of the above.

=org_capture= takes =(title target tags note)=. On 2026-08-20 it was called five times with two invented arguments, =todo_state= and =body=. Every call succeeded, returned /"Captured: ... (ID: ...)"/, and silently dropped both.

*The measured damage, all in one session.* Three headings landed with no TODO keyword. *Four landed with no body at all* -- =41cf5830=, =c6fc6f46=, =5a5e87c9= and this one -- losing every word of analysis written into them. Only =a729c32a= survived, and only because =org_amend= was called on it later for an unrelated reason.

*Neither loss announced itself.* A keywordless heading is absent from every agenda TODO view, invisible to =org_query todo:TODO=, uncounted by its parent's statistics cookie, and ignored by =--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo=. An empty body looks exactly like a heading someone has not written up yet. Both were reported to the user as filed work, with content quoted back that was never in the file.

*It went unnoticed for five hours and was found by accident* -- the user asked where a concept had been parked, and the heading holding it was empty. Nothing in the tool's replies, the lint run, the test suite or the review queue could have surfaced it, because from every one of those angles a heading with a title and an =:ID:= is well-formed.

*The tool was not at fault and this is the point.* Its description says, in capitals, that the heading is written with /NO TODO keyword/ and that the state must be set afterwards with =org_set_todo=; it advertises no =body= parameter at all. The documentation was correct, prominent, and unread. Only the silence made the error survivable. A parameter the schema does not declare should be an error, not a no-op -- the same argument =org_set_todo= already accepts for an undeclared keyword, which returns /"WAIT is not a TODO keyword in this file (have: ...)"/ rather than proceeding.

*Where the check belongs is the open question.* If =claude-code-ide-mcp= dispatches by arity or by plist, an unknown key may be discarded before this module ever sees it, in which case this is an upstream report ([[id:9d6449c7-529f-49a0-b28f-015cce869715][9d6449c7]]) rather than a local fix. Establish which before proposing anything.

*Related defect found in the same reading:* =org_capture='s own description contained a truncated sentence -- /"Run org_outline first if you need to see what "/ ran straight into /"Returns a confirmation containing the "/, losing "categories exist." Repaired 2026-08-20. Evidence for this parent's premise that the advertising has never been read end to end.

/Body restored 2026-08-20 by =org_amend=; the original capture silently discarded it -- by the very defect this heading describes./

*Gating question answered 2026-08-20: the drop is upstream, so this is a report, not a local fix.* =claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server--validate-args= iterates over =arg-specs= -- the declared schema -- and pulls each declared name out of the supplied =args= alist. It never iterates =args=. A key the schema does not declare is therefore never looked at, and silence is structural rather than a missing check:

: (dolist (spec arg-specs (nreverse result))
:   (let* ((name (plist-get spec :name))
:          (optional (plist-get spec :optional))
:          (value (alist-get (intern name) args)))
:     (when (and (not optional) (not value))
:       (signal 'json-rpc-error (list -32602 (format "Missing required argument: %s" name))))
:     (push value result)))

Note the asymmetry: a *missing required* argument signals =-32602=, so the machinery for refusing a malformed call already exists and is used. Only the unknown-key direction is unguarded. That makes the upstream report a small one -- one pass over =args= for keys absent from =arg-specs= -- rather than a design change. Belongs with [[id:9d6449c7-529f-49a0-b28f-015cce869715][9d6449c7]] alongside the =200=-versus-=202= finding.

There is a second dispatch path, =claude-code-ide-mcp.el= line 352, which hands the whole alist to a handler; it was not examined. Establish whether it has the same gap before writing the report.

*The same reading turned up a live trap in this project's own schema, now fixed.* Keys are matched verbatim -- =(intern name)= against the JSON key -- so the advertised spelling is what a caller must send. =org_sort_children= advertised =sort-type= with a hyphen while every other multi-word argument in all fourteen tools uses snake_case. A model sending the conventional =sort_type= would have had it silently discarded, which is this heading's failure mode arriving by a different door. Renamed to =sort_type= 2026-08-20.

*The rest of the surface is clean, which is worth recording so it is not re-audited.* All fourteen tools were checked mechanically, comparing each advertised argument name against its handler's arglist: *zero mismatches* once snake_case is normalised to kebab-case. Every advertised parameter exists and every real parameter is advertised. The five failures on 2026-08-20 -- =todo_state=, =body=, =file=, =max_level=, and =body= again -- were invented wholesale, not misread from a bad schema. Fixing descriptions would not have prevented any of them.

*One measured consequence beyond the lost bodies:* an =org_outline= call passing =file= and =max_level= returned the *unrestricted default* rather than the scoped view it appeared to return, and was reported as scoped. A dropped argument does not merely fail to apply -- it silently substitutes a different answer to a different question.

Observed 2026-08-19: an apply pass refused
[[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]]'s =none -> TODO=
because =--at-id= could not find the heading. The heading existed; the
index did not know where.

*Cause.* It was captured with no target, so
[[id:97696fc2-f07b-4586-b280-de6a7d26fb87][97696fc2]] put it in
=~/org/notes.org=, and it was then moved into =TODO.org= by cutting and
re-inserting the subtree text. That never touches =org-id-locations=, so
=org-id-find= kept resolving to a file the heading had left.

*Wider than its cause.* Any hand move between files does this — a
=git= operation, an =Edit= tool call, a refile done as text. Only
=org-refile= maintains the index, and this project edits org text
directly all the time.

*Candidate fixes, none chosen.* Call =org-id-update-id-locations= after
any tool that relocates a subtree; or have =--at-id= fall back to a scan
of the tracked files when the index misses, which is self-healing and
costs a scan only on the failing path. The second also covers moves made
outside Emacs entirely, which the first cannot.

*Left as a question:* whether the =Error:= that reaches the review
buffer should distinguish "no such :ID: anywhere" from "not where the
index says", since the two want different responses and currently read
identically.

:CREATED:  [2026-08-19 Wed 08:06]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-19 Wed 08:02]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:08] =>  0:06
- [2026-08-19 Wed 08:02]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:08] assigned at review
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-19 Wed 08:07] \\
  reconciliation check is the durable fix
CLOCK: [2026-08-19 Wed 08:20]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:22] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-19 Wed 08:20]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:22] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-19 Wed 08:28]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:29] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-19 Wed 08:28]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:29] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-19 Wed 08:42]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:46] =>  0:04
- [2026-08-19 Wed 08:42]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:46] assigned at review
:END:

*Incident, 2026-08-19.* Five queued transitions were applied by the
human, marked consumed in the watermark, and then destroyed —
=09c134c4= -> DONE, =507754ba= -> DONE, =7d739afd= -> TODO,
=9202b39d= -> TODO, and =507754ba= -> DOING. The queue said they were
applied; the file never carried them; =git log -S= confirms the keywords
reached no commit at all. Discovered only because someone asked whether
there were open loops.

*Cause.* Claude ran =git checkout -- TODO.org= to undo a botched edit of
its own, having first run =git status --porcelain=, seen =M TODO.org=,
and assumed the modification was entirely its own. It was not: the same
working-tree change also held the human's applied review results from
after the previous commit. One command reverted both.

*Why nothing caught it.* The watermark lives in =~/.claude/org-updates/=
and the record lives in git, so discarding the second leaves the first
claiming work that no longer exists. Nothing reconciles them, and the
review buffer reads the watermark — so the lost transitions did not
reappear as pending. They were invisible in both directions: gone from
the file, and gone from the queue's idea of what is outstanding.

This is the same class as the note already in CLAUDE.md's memory about
never running a pathspec-less =git commit=, and it generalises: *the
working tree is shared*, so any command that discards uncommitted state
— =checkout=, =restore=, =stash=, =reset --hard= — must be preceded by
reading the diff, not by assuming authorship of it.

*Recovered* by re-queueing the four transitions that mattered. The
=DOING= step of =507754ba= was not re-queued: it is now DONE, and
replaying an intermediate state would write a State line for a
transition that never happened at that time.

*Not recovered:* the =- State ...= log lines the original apply wrote,
which carried the real timestamps. The re-queued events carry today's.

*Candidate fixes, none chosen.* A reconciliation check that reports
watermark entries whose effect is absent from the file would have caught
this the moment it happened, and is the only option that catches it
whoever the culprit is. Cheaper and narrower: a hook or a documented
rule that refuses to discard =TODO.org=/=DONE.org= working-tree changes
without showing the diff first.

  decide what is worth capturing at all
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-18, in these words: /"I am questioning the
long-term utility of capturing this quantity of minutiae."/ The trigger
was reading [[id:226ed53b-c302-4529-870f-a42962b50bde][226ed53b]] and
saying, plainly, /"I can't grok it."/

*The case study.* 226ed53b was created at 07:20 and by 17:00 its body
was *274 lines*, written by Claude in four sittings on one day:

| section                                  | lines | written |
|------------------------------------------+-------+---------|
| original diagnosis                       |   ~85 | 07:20   |
| "Directions, none chosen" (3 options)    |   ~12 | 07:20   |
| 2(a) root-cause analysis                 |   ~60 | 10:00   |
| 2(b) shipping journal                    |   ~55 | 11:00   |
| 2(c) shipping journal                    |   ~70 | 16:00   |

No summary anywhere. A reader hit ninety lines of "here is what is
broken and what we might do" before reaching "here is what we did", and
the section that looked most like a decision point — "Directions, none
chosen" — was false by then, since the first direction had shipped as
2(c). A stale claim ("the mechanism does still exist") sat mid-body,
true for one morning.

*What went wrong is structural, not stylistic, and worth naming
precisely:*

1. =org_amend= only *appends*. Every correction and every summary
   therefore lands at the bottom, furthest from where a reader starts.
   The tool's shape produced the document's shape. Inserting the
   summary at the top required a hand edit through =emacsclient=.
2. *Nothing ever marks a passage superseded.* The convention says a body
   is a journal, which accumulates — but a journal of a *live* task also
   contains predictions and open options, and those go stale silently
   while the accumulation rule protects them from deletion.
3. *The volume is generated by the party that does not have to read
   it.* Claude writes these; the user reads them. Every incentive that
   would normally bound length is missing.

*The real question, which this heading exists to answer and which should
not be answered by whoever touches it first:* what is actually worth
keeping? Candidates, none chosen —

- /Everything, but structured/: summary first, mandatory, with the
  narrative below it. Cheapest, and does not reduce volume at all.
- /Outcome and falsification only/: what shipped, what was measured,
  what turned out wrong. Drop the reasoning that led nowhere. Risks
  losing the "three simpler versions were wrong first" material, which
  is the most reused thing in this repo.
- /A length budget per heading/, forcing a decision about what earns its
  place. Arbitrary, but arbitrary limits are how most writing gets
  edited.
- /Split journal from record/: a short heading body, with the long-form
  narrative in the linked plan file or a separate archive.

*Evidence to gather before deciding*, since this is exactly the kind of
question that should not be settled by taste alone: how many of these
bodies has anyone reread? =DONE.org= is 9000+ lines. A defensible
starting measure is how often an older heading's body is cited by a
later one — this repo does that constantly via =[[id:...]]= links, and
those links are countable. If long bodies are cited and short ones are
not, the volume is earning its keep; if the citations all point at the
same handful of paragraphs, it is not.

*Related:* the same failure has now appeared twice in one day at
different scales — a plan file whose claims were falsified during
execution and never revised
([[id:507754ba-ae82-4f04-b209-8d8c58ac268e][507754ba]] and 226ed53b both
corrected plan claims in code rather than in the plan), and a heading
body whose early sections were falsified by its own later ones. The
mechanism is identical: append-only records with no supersession marker.

*Scope, corrected by the user 2026-08-19:* this is about *body prose*,
not =:LOGBOOK:= entries. The drawers are machine-written, bounded by the
work that happened, and now shorter per line than before
[[id:507754ba-ae82-4f04-b209-8d8c58ac268e][507754ba]] — they are not the
problem and should not be swept up by whatever this heading decides.
What is at issue is the hand-written narrative underneath: the four
sittings of prose in the table above, none of which any tool bounds.

*Scope again, corrected a second time 2026-08-19.* This is about *body
prose per heading* — not the number of headings, and not =:LOGBOOK:=.
The user's position on heading count is the opposite: /"a high number of
well-bounded tasks seems like a good thing, especially if we
aggressively gather them into coherent containers that can be planned
and executed as larger sets of such tasks."/

So the target is the wrong one if any candidate answer reduces *how
many* headings get filed. Filing more, smaller, sharper headings is the
desired direction; writing four sittings of narrative into one of them
is not.

That also supplies the criterion this heading was missing. The stated
goal is *longer planning turns and longer implementation turns, with
fewer context switches through the day* — so prose earns its place when
it lets a future session pick a container up and run it without
re-deriving the context, and fails when it is a narrative of how the
last session got there. "What would the next person need to start" is a
sharper test than any length budget, and unlike a budget it does not
punish the measurements, which are the part most often reused.

*Do not answer this heading without asking first.* The user has a new
task lifecycle in mind (stated 2026-08-19, deliberately not elaborated
at the time). It very likely determines what a body is *for*, and
therefore what belongs in one — so any answer composed before hearing it
is a guess that will need redoing.

Ask for it before picking this up.

*Largely answered 2026-08-20.* The user supplied the retention policy this heading was waiting on, recorded in full at [[id:3063c3e5-0dd0-4354-8985-271b0313c5d1][3063c3e5]] because it arrived as a decision about =org_amend=. In short: an open body holds the problem, the latest intended approach and verified facts; a closed body holds a concise debrief; the original problem statement and plan move into a drawer; git holds the evolution, indexed from a drawer rather than narrated in the body.

*What remains here is the retrofit, which is the harder half.* The policy is prospective by construction — CLAUDE.md already forbids trimming bodies written before 2026-08-14 — so this heading is now about the boundary: which existing bodies get rewritten when they are next touched, and which are left as they are. It is blocked on [[id:3063c3e5-0dd0-4354-8985-271b0313c5d1][3063c3e5]], since no tool can rewrite a body today.

*Note this heading is not ungated on the \"task lifecycle\" the user has twice mentioned.* The retention policy is one part of that lifecycle, not the whole of it; the transition rules discussed the same day (a clock starting only at a leaf, a container leaving DOING when all children are terminal) are another part and are recorded against [[id:241a4c54-ed59-4780-b602-2a86efee978a][241a4c54]].


* DOING [10/13] Make the guidepost pipeline produce durations that mean what they say :code:

:CREATED:  [2026-08-18 Tue 08:15]
:END:

- State "PLANNING"   from "TODO"       [2026-08-18 Tue 08:22] \\
  plan the pipeline epic and its four children
- [2026-08-18 Tue 08:38]--[2026-08-18 Tue 08:38] plan span pipeline end to end
CLOCK: [2026-08-18 Tue 08:42]--[2026-08-18 Tue 08:43] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-18 Tue 08:42]--[2026-08-18 Tue 08:43] plan span pipeline end to end
- State "DOING"      from "PLANNING"   [2026-08-18 Tue 08:54]
- [2026-08-18 Tue 08:56]--[2026-08-18 Tue 08:56] plan span pipeline end to end
CLOCK: [2026-08-18 Tue 09:32]--[2026-08-18 Tue 09:33] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-18 Tue 09:32]--[2026-08-18 Tue 09:33] plan span pipeline end to end
CLOCK: [2026-08-18 Tue 09:52]--[2026-08-18 Tue 09:54] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-18 Tue 09:52]--[2026-08-18 Tue 09:54] plan span pipeline end to end
CLOCK: [2026-08-18 Tue 10:01]--[2026-08-18 Tue 10:03] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-18 Tue 10:01]--[2026-08-18 Tue 10:03] plan span pipeline end to end
:END:

A span's duration is currently wrong in both directions, and the error
enters at three separate stages. Measured 2026-08-17 across all 834
post-cutover guideposts: kind-blind clustering totals *39.10 h* against
*21.59 h* for =resume= -> =pause= pairing — an overcount of 17.50 h, about
45%, because every idle gap under twenty minutes is absorbed into the
surrounding span. In the other direction, six turns exceeded the
threshold and were rendered as zero-width points, 4.88 h invisible.

*The three stages, which is why this is a parent rather than one fix:*

- *Input* — guideposts that record something that did not happen. A
  background task completing re-invokes the model, fires
  =UserPromptSubmit=, and appends a =resume= with no user present.
- *Transform* — =--aggregate-guideposts= maps =:ts= over events and never
  reads =:kind:=, so it clusters an undifferentiated stream. But
  =pause= -> =resume= means nobody was there and =resume= -> =pause=
  means one turn was running; the threshold was tuned for the first and
  inverts on the second. Separately, =--trigger-auto-clock-in= opens a
  live clock that nothing stops, so two mechanisms write CLOCK lines
  with no knowledge of each other.
- *Output* — a lone timestamp becomes a zero-width item that can only be
  dismissed. That is the transform's failure made visible, not a
  cosmetic annoyance.
- *Record* — 98 post-cutover CLOCK lines across TODO.org and DONE.org
  carry the accumulated error.

*Why now, and not later.* The bias profile inverts under the working mode
this project is moving toward. Long unattended stretches mean fewer turn
boundaries, so the overcount shrinks and the long-turn undercount grows —
the defect becomes dominant exactly when the human is least able to notice
it, and the work least observed becomes the work least accurately
recorded. That is the opposite of what CLAUDE.md's second stated goal
asks for.

*Deliberately not a child:
[[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]].* It carries a
=+1m= repeater and never reaches =DONE=, and its own body records being
promoted out of an epic for exactly that reason — "a recurring
measurement has no terminal state and therefore no completable parent".
Making it a child here would repeat the trap
[[id:38b92521-bba4-4bb5-9cd9-465862471a72][38b92521]] sat in. It is
referenced instead, and its subject changes if this work lands: the
threshold is not the lever once the aggregator reads =:kind:=, so what
that recurring measurement is *for* needs restating.

[[file:~/.claude/plans/elegant-questing-lamport.md][Plan]] — approved 2026-08-18. Covers all five
children; each links this same file rather than carrying one of its
own, following 02aaae22 / c556a9ed.


** DONE Background-task notifications write phantom resume guideposts :code:

:PROPERTIES:
